Discrete tokenization. ## 3 Method

Di<sub>g</sub>itC<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s a</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y o</sub>f <sub>sym</sub>b<sub>o</sub>li<sub>c co</sub>d<sub>es on</sub> HL’<sub>s gr</sub>id<sub>,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>ilt b<sub>y c</sub>h<sub>ang</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26’<sub>s</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>ons one a</sub>t <sub>a</sub> ti<sub>me: we reca</sub>ll HL<sub>-</sub>26<sub>, measure w</sub>h<sub>ere a</sub> b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er co</sub>d<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld dif<sub>er,</sub> th<sub>en ma</sub>k<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e c</sub>h<sub>anges</sub> i<sub>n</sub> t<sub>urn.</sub>

For each hand, HL extracts 20 bone-direction vectors from 3D joints and normalizes them to the unit sphere $S ^ { 2 }$ <sub>.</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26 <sub>quan</sub>ti<sub>zes a</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> $\mathbf { v } \in S ^ { 2 } \mathrm { t o } q ( \mathbf { v } ) = \underset { \mathbf { \sigma } } { \mathrm { a r g m a x } } _ { \mathbf { c } \in \mathcal { C } _ { 2 6 } } \mathbf { v } ^ { \top } \mathbf { c } .$ <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\mathcal { C } _ { 2 6 }$ <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>zes</sub> $\{ - 1 , 0 , 1 \} ^ { \bar { 3 } } \backslash \{ 0 \}$ (the cube’s 6 faces, 12 edges, 8 corners). Stacking two hands over $T$ f<sub>rames</sub> <sub>y</sub>i<sub>e</sub>ld<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> $T \times 4 0$ <sub>sym</sub>b<sub>o</sub>li<sub>c</sub> <sub>gr</sub>id <sub>we</sub> b<sub>u</sub>ild <sub>on.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ru</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>repro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ibl<sub>e</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> <sub>any</sub> <sub>pu</sub>bli<sub>c</sub> <sub>source</sub> <sub>o</sub>f 3D joints, so HL-26 needs none of HL’s internal data.

Three empirical observations on InterHand2.6M (36 captures, 717K vectors) motivate the design, each pointing to one departure from HL-26’s fixed per-bone grid. (1) Anisotropy: 68.1% of all bone directions fall on the single $+ Y$ f<sub>ace</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>cu</sub>b<sub>e, so</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26’<sub>s un</sub>if<sub>orm a</sub>l<sub>p</sub>h<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>t <sub>was</sub>t<sub>es mos</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f it<sub>s sym-</sub> bols and should instead befit to the data (App. C). (2) Intrafinger coupling: mutual information averages 39% within a finger vs. 29% across (cross-finger peak 51%, middle–ring), so the finger, not the bone, is the natural token boundary— strong dependence inside to exploit, a weak seam to cut. (3) Temporal redundancy: 79% of tokens are unchanged from the previous frame, so the time axis is compressible. A).

Di<sub>g</sub>itCode makes these three chan<sub>g</sub>es in turn (Fi<sub>g</sub>. 1): DigitCode-A fits the codebook to the data. DigitCode-F moves the unit from the bone to the finger. ## 3.1 DigitCode-A: Data-Adaptive Codebook on $S ^ { 2 }$

Th<sub>e sma</sub>ll<sub>es</sub>t d<sub>epar</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> k<sub>eeps</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>one un</sub>it <sub>an</sub>d <sub>rep</sub>l<sub>aces on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> the fixed al<sub>p</sub>habet (chan<sub>g</sub>e 1). Di<sub>g</sub>itCode-A runs s<sub>p</sub>herical kmeans (Llo<sub>y</sub>d 1982) on trainin<sub>g</sub> directions and quantizes b<sub>y</sub> <sub>argmax-cos</sub>i<sub>ne exac</sub>tl<sub>y as</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26<sub>;</sub> th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on space</sub> i<sub>s</sub> l<sub>ow-</sub> dimensional, so no encoder trainin<sub>g</sub> is needed (§7). At the same codebook size (K=26, both costing 4.70 fixed-width bits/bone), the ada<sub>p</sub>tive code attains $8 . 4 5 ^ { \circ }$ , a 43% reduction <sub>over</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26’<sub>s</sub> $1 4 . 7 \dot { 1 } ^ { \circ }$ (low-rate o<sub>p</sub>eratin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>oints in A<sub>pp</sub>. I).

## 3.2 DigitCode-F: Per-Finger Joint Quantization

The next chan<sub>g</sub>e is the unit itself (chan<sub>g</sub>e 2): from the bone t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>nger.</sub> R<sub>a</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an quan</sub>ti<sub>ze eac</sub>h <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> fi<sub>nger</sub>’<sub>s</sub> f<sub>our</sub> b<sub>ones</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> Di<sub>g</sub>itC<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e-</sub>F <sub>conca</sub>t<sub>ena</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>em</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> single 12-D vector and quantizes the finger jointly; fingers <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ay separa</sub>t<sub>e, cu</sub>tti<sub>ng a</sub>l<sub>ong</sub> th<sub>e wea</sub>k <sub>cross-</sub>fi<sub>nger seam.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> seam being anatomical is what pays, not the larger block: at fixed 12-D block, K, and rate, regrouping the same bones at random costs 2.4<sup>◦</sup> (9.48 vs. 7.10<sup>◦</sup>, K=64), a half-anatomical s<sub>p</sub>lit landin<sub>g</sub> between the two—the classical block efect (Jé- gou, Douze, and Schmid 2011) is secondary to which bones share a code (A<sub>pp</sub>. Switching the unit lowers error and rate t<sub>oge</sub>th<sub>er, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>no quan</sub>ti<sub>zer c</sub>h<sub>ange a</sub>t <sub>a</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>un</sub>it <sub>ac</sub>hi<sub>eves,</sub> and it holds under either rate convention (entropy: 3.29 bits for HL’s 14.71<sup>◦</sup> vs. 1.94 for F’s 5.50<sup>◦</sup>).

Id<sub>en</sub>tit<sub>y rea</sub>d<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e w</sub>h<sub>o</sub>l<sub>e</sub> h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>.</sub> A <sub>ges</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> i<sub>s rea</sub>d <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>coarse s</sub>ilh<sub>oue</sub>tt<sub>e, no</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>any</sub> b<sub>one, an</sub>d th<sub>e ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t b<sub>uys</sub> is the striking part: on subject-disjoint 137-class classification a single whole-hand token reaches 46.8% top-1, matching the 20-token per-bone code’s 46.2% at 1/14.6 of its rate. DigitCode-F matches HL-26 at 45% of its rate, and adding the whole-hand token reaches 49.7% (+6.7pp, CI [+4.3, +9.0])—the units are complementary. The tokens also beat MANO and joint-angle features under a fixed probe (A<sub>pp</sub>. K).

## A Reproducibility and Implementation Details

Data. We use the full InterHand2.6M (Moon et al. 2020) (36 captures, 717K bone-direction vectors from two-hand sequences) and the full FreiHAND (Zimmermann et al. 2019) (32,560 single-hand poses). For each hand we extract 20 re-<sub>g</sub>ional bone-direction vectors followin<sub>g</sub> HL (Li et al. 2024) <sub>an</sub>d <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub> th<sub>em</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e un</sub>it <sub>sp</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $S ^ { \stackrel { \triangledown } { 2 } }$ <sub>.</sub> St<sub>ac</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>s</sub> per frame yields the T × 40 grid. Figure 3 shows a sample gesture as the underlying 21-joint 3D skeleton.

Splits. All evaluations are held-out and disjoint by capt<sub>ure.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>H<sub>an</sub>d2<sub>.</sub>6M <sub>we</sub> fit <sub>on even-</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>exe</sub>d <sub>cap</sub>t<sub>ures an</sub>d test on odd-indexed ca<sub>p</sub>tures (and the reverse for s<sub>y</sub>mmetr<sub>y</sub> checks); for se<sub>q</sub>uence tasks we use seqs[::2] for trainin<sub>g</sub> and seqs[1::2] for testin<sub>g</sub>. Ada<sub>p</sub>tive codebooks are fit on th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng par</sub>titi<sub>on on</sub>l<sub>y an</sub>d <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> h<sub>e</sub>ld<sub>-ou</sub>t <sub>par-</sub> titi<sub>on; we never use a ran</sub>d<sub>om per-</sub>f<sub>rame sp</sub>lit<sub>, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>wou</sub>ld leak near-duplicate adjacent frames. Gesture classification uses a stricter split: the oficial subject annotations show that subjects span multiple captures (e.g. subject 0 appears in Captures 0, 4, and 17; subject 1 in Captures 1 and 2), so an <sub>even</sub>/<sub>o</sub>dd <sub>cap</sub>t<sub>ure sp</sub>lit l<sub>ea</sub>k<sub>s s</sub>i<sub>gner</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tit<sub>y</sub> f<sub>or c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on;</sub> we therefore assign 14 subjects to train and 13 to test with zero overlap and quote only this subject-disjoint split.

N).

Coupling statistics. Mutual information between bone tokens rises along a finger’s kinematic chain, reaching 59% toward the fingertip; cross-finger MI peaks at 51% (middle– ring) with the thumb most independent (∼27%), and averages 39% within a finger vs. 29% across fingers. Temporally, 79% of HL-26 tokens and 66% of DigitCode-A (K=64) tokens <sub>are unc</sub>h<sub>ange</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e prev</sub>i<sub>ous</sub> f<sub>rame—s</sub>t<sub>rong</sub> d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>ence</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>id<sub>e a</sub> fi<sub>nger</sub> t<sub>o exp</sub>l<sub>o</sub>it<sub>, a wea</sub>k <sub>seam</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> fi<sub>ngers</sub> t<sub>o cu</sub>t<sub>,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d h<sub>eavy</sub> f<sub>rame-</sub>t<sub>o-</sub>f<sub>rame re</sub>d<sub>un</sub>d<sub>ancy</sub> t<sub>o compress.</sub> Th<sub>e pa</sub>i<sub>r-</sub> wise numbers are corroborated by a partition-level statistic (Sec. One operating point, two conventions. B) uses 10,000 boot-<sub>s</sub>t<sub>rap</sub>/<sub>permu</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>rep</sub>li<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>es;</sub> <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e-run.</sub>

Model hyperparameters and seeds. All sequence mod-<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s s</sub>h<sub>are one con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>a pr</sub>i<sub>or</sub>i <sub>an</sub>d <sub>no</sub>t t<sub>une</sub>d per task: a LLaMA-style decoder with 6 layers, hidden size 320, 10 attention heads, intermediate size 4×, and R<sub>o</sub>PE $\theta \ = \ 1 0 ^ { 4 }$ , trained with AdamW (lr $3 ~ \times ~ 1 0 ^ { - 4 }$ weight decay 0.01), a cosine schedule with 3% warmup, and batch size 32. The codebook size K is the only swe<sub>p</sub>t h<sub>yp</sub>er<sub>p</sub>arameter (<sub>p</sub>er-bone $K \in \{ 2 6 , 6 4 , 1 2 8 \}$ , p<sup>er-</sup> fi<sub>nger</sub> $\bar { K } \in \{ 6 4 , 1 2 8 , 2 5 6 \}$ hi<sub>erarc</sub>hi<sub>ca</sub>l $( K _ { 1 } , \dot { K _ { 2 } } ) ~ =$ $( 1 \bar { 2 } 8 , \{ 8 , 1 6 , 3 2 \bar { \} } ) )$ <sub>, se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>-ra</sub>t<sub>e compar</sub>i<sub>son</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> R<sub>–</sub>D t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es;</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>repa</sub>i<sub>r-</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>ace</sub> <sub>conc</sub>l<sub>us</sub>i<sub>ons</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>ec</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d for stability over K ∈ {64, 128, 256} (Sec. O). Randomness polic<sub>y</sub>: single-run anal<sub>y</sub>ses fix seed 0 throughout (NumP<sub>y</sub> default\_rng(0), torch.manual\_seed(0)); the <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-see</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>u</sub>di<sub>es</sub> <sub>a</sub>b<sub>ove</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>ree</sub> di<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>nc</sub>t i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>eger</sub> seeds and re<sub>p</sub>ort mean±std; the motion-AE behind AE-FGD is trained once at seed 0 and shared by all arms of a study.

Quantizers. Spherical k-means uses cosine assignment <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a mean-</sub>th<sub>en-renorma</sub>li<sub>ze up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e;</sub> k<sub>-me</sub>d<sub>o</sub>id<sub>s res</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>t<sub>s</sub> centers to observed directions. Learned VQ/RVQ use a strai<sub>g</sub>ht-throu<sub>g</sub>h estimator with codebook EMA; FSQ (Mentzer et al. 2024), PQ (Jé<sub>g</sub>ou, Douze, and Schmid 2011), and BSQ (Zhao, Xion<sub>g</sub>, and Krähenbühl 2024) follow their <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d f<sub>ormu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> F<sub>or au</sub>t<sub>oregress</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>orecas</sub>ti<sub>ng an</sub>d <sub>g</sub>eneration we train a small LLaMA-st<sub>y</sub>le transformer (Touvron et al. 2023) over the token <sub>g</sub>rid; the masked decoder follows MoMask/MMM-st<sub>y</sub>le confidence decodin<sub>g</sub> (Guo et al. 2024; Pin<sub>y</sub>oanunta<sub>p</sub>on<sub>g</sub> et al. 2024).

Counting instead by quantized codeword—the fraction whose HL-26 code is one of the nine alphabet entries with y>0—gives 82.4%; we report th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ace-ass</sub>i<sub>gnmen</sub>t <sub>num</sub>b<sub>er</sub> b<sub>ecause</sub> it <sub>measures</sub> th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a,</sub> <sub>no</sub>t th<sub>e a</sub>l<sub>p</sub>h<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Eith<sub>er way</sub> th<sub>e conc</sub>l<sub>us</sub>i<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e one</sub> Fi<sub>g.</sub> 4 <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ows: a un</sub>if<sub>orm a</sub>l<sub>p</sub>h<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>t <sub>spen</sub>d<sub>s mos</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f it<sub>s sym</sub>b<sub>o</sub>l<sub>s w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>ere</sub> i<sub>s a</sub>l<sub>mos</sub>t <sub>no</sub>thi<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>o enco</sub>d<sub>e.</sub>

Why naive spherical coordinates underperform. The <sub>na</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>sp</sub>h<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l<sub>-gr</sub>id <sub>row</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s</sub> b<sub>ecause</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>se</sub>t<sub>up,</sub> <sub>no</sub>t b<sub>e-</sub> <sub>cause</sub> <sub>sp</sub>h<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>coor</sub>di<sub>na</sub>t<sub>es</sub> <sub>are</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>poor.</sub> A <sub>na</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> (θ, ϕ) grid places its pole on the +Z axis—the sparsest di-<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>or</sub> h<sub>an</sub>d b<sub>ones—so ce</sub>ll<sub>s crow</sub>d <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>ere</sub> i<sub>s no</sub> data (42–50% dead codes at $K { = } 1 2 8 / 2 4 )$ <sub>.</sub> T<sub>wo</sub> fi<sub>xes recover</sub> most of the gap: moving the pole to +Y (the dense “forward” axis) im<sub>p</sub>roves the error from $1 5 . 3 5 ^ { \circ }$ to $1 0 . 4 1 ^ { \circ }$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d switching to equal-area cells improves it further to 10.27<sup>◦</sup> (a separate pole-and-cell-shape sweep at higher K, not the K=40 row of Table 8), comparable to Fibonacci sampling. At th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>ow-ra</sub>t<sub>e en</sub>d th<sub>e a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>ti<sub>ve co</sub>d<sub>e nee</sub>d<sub>s very</sub> littl<sub>e</sub> b<sub>u</sub>d<sub>-</sub> get: per-bone k-means at K=12 (3.59 bits) already reaches 12.7<sup>◦</sup>, below HL-26 at fewer bits.

We hold the block size (5 groups × 4 bones = 12-D), the codebook size K, the fitting pipeline, the split, and the metric fixed, and vary only which bones share a group: within-finger (DigitCode-F itself); cross-finger, joint level j of index/middle/ring/pinky with the thumb kept whole— deliberately half-anatomical; diagonal, a Latin square in which group g takes joint j from finger (g+j) mod 5, so <sub>every group spans</sub> f<sub>our</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t fi<sub>ngers an</sub>d f<sub>our</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t joint levels; and ten draws of random disjoint quartets. A joint-level partition (4 groups × 5 bones) is reported apart b<sub>ecause</sub> it<sub>s</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>kl<sub>eng</sub>th dif<sub>ers.</sub> Th<sub>e w</sub>ithi<sub>n-</sub>fi<sub>nger arm re-</sub> <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uces</sub> Di<sub>g</sub>itC<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e-</sub>F’<sub>s</sub> <sub>pu</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>opera</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>ree</sub> decimals (7.100<sup>◦</sup> at 1.46 bits/bone for $K { = } 6 4 ; 5 . 5 0 4 ^ { \circ }$ at 1.94 for K=256), which anchors the control to the main paper’s <sub>num</sub>b<sub>ers ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> t<sub>o a re-</sub>i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

The anatomical partition wins by 1.8–2.6<sup>◦</sup> at K=64 (2.4– 2.5<sup>◦</sup> against the random and diagonal arms, 1.8<sup>◦</sup> against the half-anatomical cross-finger one) and 1.6–2.2<sup>◦</sup> at K=256, and sits below the best of ten random draws at both sizes $( 9 . 0 7 ^ { \circ }$ and 7.16<sup>◦</sup>)—roughly ten standard deviations below th<sub>e ran</sub>d<sub>om mean.</sub> Bl<sub>oc</sub>kl<sub>eng</sub>th <sub>canno</sub>t b<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e mec</sub>h<sub>an</sub>i<sub>sm:</sub> every arm above the rule quantizes the same 12-D blocks at the same K and within 0.03 bits of the same rate. What <sub>separa</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>em</sub> i<sub>s a</sub> d<sub>ose–response</sub> i<sub>n</sub> h<sub>ow muc</sub>h <sub>ana</sub>t<sub>omy</sub> survives: kee<sub>p</sub>in<sub>g</sub> one fin<sub>g</sub>er intact (cross-fin<sub>g</sub>er) recovers <sub>a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>a</sub> thi<sub>r</sub>d <sub>o</sub>fth<sub>e gap</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y ana</sub>t<sub>om</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>par</sub>titi<sub>on, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>agona</sub>l <sub>arm—</sub>b<sub>u</sub>ilt t<sub>o sever every</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ra-</sub>fi<sub>nger c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n—</sub>i<sub>s</sub> the worst 12-D arm at both K. The blocklength efect is real and is what lifts every 12-D arm above a per-bone code; the <sub>ana</sub>t<sub>om</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>group</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>s w</sub>h<sub>a</sub>t t<sub>urns</sub> it f<sub>rom a mo</sub>d<sub>es</sub>t <sub>ga</sub>i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> the frontier. The joint-level arm is not directly comparable (lar<sub>g</sub>er blocks, fewer tokens, lower rate), but its orderin<sub>g</sub> is consistent: grouping the same joint across fingers is worse t<sup>h</sup>an <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub><sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub> a <sup>fi</sup>n<sub>g</sub>er.

Spatial, not temporal, decoupling (boundary result). W<sub>e</sub> t<sub>es</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> l<sub>ayers</sub> f<sub>orm</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>s</sub>l<sub>ow–</sub>f<sub>as</sub>t t<sub>empora</sub>l d<sub>ecompos</sub>iti<sub>on.</sub> Th<sub>ey</sub> d<sub>o no</sub>t<sub>:</sub> th<sub>e coarse an</sub>d <sub>res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l l<sub>ayers</sub> change at almost the same per-frame rate (47% vs. 48%). ## F Relative Encoding and Temporal Structure

The grid is visibly redundant. Figures 6 and 7 render a 110-frame two-hand window as the T × 40 HL-26 grid <sub>an</sub>d it<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rame-</sub>t<sub>o-</sub>f<sub>rame c</sub>h<sub>ange map, ma</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e re</sub>d<sub>un</sub>d<sub>ancy</sub> <sub>quan</sub>tifi<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ow</sub> <sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ibl<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub> <sub>g</sub>l<sub>ance.</sub>

Relative encoding. Encoding directions relative to a refer-<sub>ence concen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>hift<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e w</sub>h<sub>o</sub>l<sub>e</sub> R<sub>–</sub>D curve left (Table 12). The mean angular distance to the +Y axis drops from 43.8<sup>◦</sup> (absolute) to 26.8<sup>◦</sup> (parent-relative) to 15.4<sup>◦</sup> (temporal-relative).

Closed-loop stability. Using reconstructed (rather than <sub>g</sub>round-truth) references at decode time, error <sub>g</sub>rowth is b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d<sub>: paren</sub>t<sub>-re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> d<sub>egra</sub>d<sub>e</sub> b<sub>y</sub> ${ \sim } + 1 ^ { \circ }$ <sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> chain end, and temporal-relative coding by +1.6–2.1<sup>◦</sup>. Both <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>usa</sub>bl<sub>e.</sub>

Temporal-relative coding subsumes compression. Two di<sub>agnos</sub>ti<sub>cs s</sub>h<sub>ow</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>ecorre</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>compress</sub>i<sub>on are</sub> the same o<sub>p</sub>eration here. (i) Multi-ste<sub>p</sub> conditional entro<sub>py</sub> $H ( t \mid t - k )$ deca<sub>y</sub>s slowl<sub>y</sub> for absolute tokens (still low at $k { = } 2 0 )$ <sub>,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t i<sub>s essen</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> fl<sub>a</sub>t f<sub>or</sub> $k > 1$ <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> t<sub>empora</sub>l<sub>-</sub> relative codin<sub>g</sub>—its residual is close to i.i.d. (ii) Of-the-shelf gzip compresses HL-26 token streams to 1.57 bits/vector, but cannot compress temporal-relative tokens at 3.37 bits at all. Relatedly, HL-26 changes only 21% of tokens frame-tof<sub>rame:</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>coarse</sub> <sub>co</sub>d<sub>e</sub> “<sub>a</sub>b<sub>sor</sub>b<sub>s</sub>” <sub>sma</sub>ll <sub>mo</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h h<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ps</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d h<sub>ur</sub>t<sub>s</sub> fi<sub>ne</sub> f<sub>orecas</sub>ti<sub>ng.</sub>

Figure 6: The HL-26 token grid as a “hand pianoroll”: 40 bone-direction channels (rows; the two hands split b<sub>y</sub> the white divider) over a 110-frame window (columns). Colour encodes the direction a token names rather than its index: lightness falls with the angle from +Y (forward) and hue is the azimuth about that axis, so neighbouring directions receive neighbouring colours and a small pose change reads as a small colour shift. The pale anchor covering most of the grid is the +Y concentration of Sec. C. Key at right: the 26 codes laced at radius = an le from +Y, azimuth = azimuth. Lon horizontal bands of constant colour—and the near-constant MCP (knuckle) rows—are the tem<sub>p</sub>oral and kinematic redundanc<sub>y</sub> a time-aware code can com<sub>p</sub>ress.

Why an anatomy-grouped delay. The delay grouping i<sub>s</sub> <sub>mo</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>ona</sub>l<sub>-en</sub>t<sub>ropy</sub> <sub>ana</sub>l<sub>ys</sub>i<sub>s</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> DigitCode-A per-bone tokens (K=64). The intra-finger kinematic chain is stron<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y</sub> directional—<sub>p</sub>arent→child mutual information rises alon the chain (0.16 for PIP|MCP, 0.43 for DIP|PIP, 0.59 for TIP|DIP)—and packing tokens by finger (within-group MI 0.39) or by joint level (0.33) both beat a cross-group baseline (0.29). This motivates grouping the d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ay</sub> b<sub>y</sub> <sub>ana</sub>t<sub>omy</sub> <sub>ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> <sub>ar</sub>bit<sub>rar</sub>il<sub>y.</sub> A b<sub>roa</sub>d<sub>er</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e-</sub> seed sweep (legacy 45-D statistic FGD, cross-seed std ±283– 424) confirms only the coarse separation robust to that noise: every anatomy-grouped delay (∼2000–2300) beats parallel (2572), random within-frame order (3261), and the flat full-AR chain (6650), preserved across K∈{32, 64, 128}. The fi<sub>ner con</sub>t<sub>ras</sub>t<sub>s—c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ay w</sub>idth<sub>—</sub>f<sub>a</sub>ll i<sub>ns</sub>id<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e see</sub>d <sub>no</sub>i<sub>se, so we</sub> d<sub>o no</sub>t <sub>c</sub>l<sub>a</sub>i<sub>m</sub> th<sub>em:</sub> th<sub>e c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> di<sub>rec-</sub> ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s a</sub>d<sub>op</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> ki<sub>nema</sub>ti<sub>c-en</sub>t<sub>ropy argumen</sub>t <sub>a</sub>b<sub>ove,</sub> and the minimal delay magnitude by cost (1.08 vs. 1.6/2.1 ste<sub>p</sub>s/frame), not b<sub>y</sub> measured qualit<sub>y</sub>.

Why generation, not teacher-forced likelihood. A teacher-forced likelihood (bits per bone-token) is not a valid <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub> f<sub>or cross-</sub>f<sub>rame</sub> d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ay sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>es.</sub> A d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>aye</sub>d b<sub>one o</sub>f frame t is decoded at a step that already conditions on earlydelay bones of future frames, and because hand motion is <sub>smoo</sub>th th<sub>ose</sub> f<sub>u</sub>t<sub>ure groun</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>ru</sub>th t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fl<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>oss—a</sub> d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ay sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>e</sub>’<sub>s</sub> b<sub>p</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> l<sub>ea</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d<sub>, no</sub>t <sub>earne</sub>d<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>e</sub>ll i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> zero-dela<sub>y p</sub>arallel confi<sub>g</sub> (no leak) loses; at the likelihood l<sub>eve</sub>l th<sub>e</sub> fl<sub>a</sub>t f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>-</sub>AR <sub>c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> th<sub>e causa</sub>l <sub>ce</sub>ili<sub>ng</sub> (leak-free, frame-causal: flat 3.24 ≈ a within-frame reorder 3.28 ≈ random 3.25, so a within-frame schedule yields no free likelihood). We therefore evaluate schedules onl<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> leak-free <sub>g</sub>eneration (FGD), as in the main <sub>p</sub>a<sub>p</sub>er.

Matched-budget control. To rule out that flat merely <sub>nee</sub>d<sub>s more</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng, we re</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n every sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> 12K-step budget, at 3 training seeds, and score leak-free gen-<sub>era</sub>ti<sub>on w</sub>ith th<sub>e co</sub>ll<sub>apse-sens</sub>iti<sub>ve mo</sub>ti<sub>on-</sub>AE l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t FGD (Sec. A). On HanCo (n=119 generated clips per configuration) the orderin<sub>g</sub> of Table 14 is identical at ever<sub>y</sub> seed.

A sin<sub>g</sub>le k-means la<sub>y</sub>er matches learned VQ at ever<sub>y</sub> rate, the hi<sub>g</sub>h-fidelit<sub>y</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ime included (2.47 vs. 2.45<sup>◦</sup> at ∼8 b), and both beat the two-stage RVQ there (2.80<sup>◦</sup> at 8.03 b); FSQ/PQ/BSQ are dominated at equal rate (BSQ reaches 10.33<sup>◦</sup> only with a 256-code book). Rate is matched empirical entropy; the ∼8b cells sit at 7.99, 8.30 and 8.03 bits respectively.

## the factorization; we use raw 3-D throughout.

The RVQ ladder. Residual VQ carries two knobs—perstage K and depth—so each of its cells above reports the best (K, stages) nearest to that column’s rate: (6, 2) at 4.33 b, (5, 3) at 5.82 b, (16, 2) at 6.61 b, (32, 2) at 8.03 b. Depth is not free where bits are scarce: at ∼5.0 bits a three-stage code is worse than a two-stage one (8.34<sup>◦</sup> at 5.04 b vs. 7.51<sup>◦</sup> at 5.06 b), because each extra stage spends a code index before th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>one</sub> h<sub>as</sub> <sub>reso</sub>l<sub>ve</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>gap</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e</sub> k<sub>-means</sub> l<sub>ayer</sub> <sub>w</sub>id<sub>ens</sub> <sub>mono</sub>t<sub>on</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>as</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> f<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>s:</sub> 0.38, 0.60, 1.59<sup>◦</sup> at ∼6.6, ∼5.6, ∼4.4 bits.

The ordering does not reverse at the top. What the spherical column is matched on. The <sub>sp</sub>h<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l<sub>-coor</sub>di<sub>na</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>co</sub>l<sub>umn</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>paper</sub>’<sub>s</sub> <sub>quan</sub>ti<sub>zer</sub> table is matched on nominal K, not on entropy. A total codebook of 64 there ((8, 2), 5.41 entropy b) gives 8.71<sup>◦</sup>, against k-means’ 6.59<sup>◦</sup> at 5.84 b.

Why FSQ and BSQ have no high-rate cell. Both place cells uniforml<sub>y</sub>, and bone directions are not uniform (Fi<sub>g</sub>. 4), <sub>so</sub> th<sub>e co</sub>d<sub>es</sub> th<sub>ey a</sub>dd l<sub>an</sub>d <sub>mos</sub>tl<sub>y w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>ere</sub> i<sub>s no</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a an</sub>d their achieved entropy falls ever further below the nomina log K they are charged. FSQ’s per-axis grid runs 2.64, 3.68, 4.48, 5.00 entropy bits at $L { = } 3 { - } 6 \ ( K { = } 2 7 { - } 2 1 6 )$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>s</sub>h<sub>or</sub>tf<sub>a</sub>ll widening from 2.11 to 2.75 bits; BSQ’s binary spherical code runs 2.54, 3.03, 4.04, 4.61, 5.10 bits at $K { = } \dot { 8 } { \mathrm { - } } \dot { 1 } 0 2 4 .$ <sub>, a s</sub>h<sub>or</sub>t<sub>-</sub> fall reaching 4.90. Geometric quantization reaches the learned bound. Table 17 runs a controlled sweep against a properly tuned learned VQ (EMA codebook with dead-code revival) at three <sub>granu</sub>l<sub>ar</sub>iti<sub>es,</sub> $K { = } 6 4$ . The two tie ever where—within 0.2<sup>◦</sup> at every granularity, both at ≈100% utilization—because the direction space is low-dimensional (participation ratio 2.8– 4.2) and simple, leaving the learned encoder little to add over Lloyd’s algorithm. We report the tuned VQ deliberately: an under-tuned VQ (no dead-code revival) colla<sub>p</sub>ses and loses, which would make geometric quantization look superior. The per-bone tie is seed-stable: across 3 VQ seeds, paired perse<sub>q</sub>uence <sup>d</sup>e<sup>l</sup>tas sta<sub>y</sub> $\leq 0 . 1 5 ^ { \circ }$ and flip sign at K=64/128 (kmeans $5 . 6 4 / 4 . 0 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { { \ w s . \ V Q \ 5 . 6 0 \pm 0 . { \dot { 0 } } 4 / { \dot { 4 } } . 0 8 \pm 0 . 0 3 } }$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e ev</sub>i<sub>-</sub> d<sub>ence</sub> f<sub>orm a c</sub>l<sub>a</sub>i<sub>me</sub>d <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>e a</sub>l<sub>so surv</sub>i<sub>ves</sub> all the wa<sub>y</sub> u<sub>p</sub> the rate axis: at ∼8 entro<sub>py</sub> bits k-means and VQ difer by 0.02<sup>◦</sup> (2.47 vs. 2.45), a fourth operating point <sub>a</sub>t <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h th<sub>e quan</sub>ti<sub>zer ru</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s nu</sub>ll<sub>.</sub> R<sub>es</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l d<sub>ep</sub>th d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>en</sub>d th<sub>e per-</sub>b<sub>one</sub> f<sub>ron</sub>ti<sub>er pas</sub>t <sub>a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e</sub> l<sub>ayer a</sub>t <sub>any ra</sub>t<sub>e we</sub> swee<sub>p</sub> (Table 15); what extends it is chan<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> the unit.

Efect decomposition: the quantizer rule is null against its noise floor. A non-significant test would be weak evidence for “the quantizer does not matter” (absence of evidence is not evidence of absence). Table 18 instead calibrates each desi<sub>g</sub>n knob a<sub>g</sub>ainst the VQ’s own run-to-run noise, and— thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e—every</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>e</sub>d <sub>row</sub> h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e ana</sub>t<sub>om</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>un</sub>it <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e mov</sub>i<sub>ng exac</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> <sub>one</sub> k<sub>no</sub>b<sub>.</sub> W<sub>e separa</sub>t<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> thi<sub>ngs</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>are o</sub>ft<sub>en con</sub>fl<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>:</sub> the quantization rule (argmax-cosine vs. k-means assignment vs. FSQ/PQ/BSQ), which is null at fixed unit and rate, and the codebook content (a fixed cube vs. a data-fit set of codewords), which is not—Di<sub>g</sub>itCode-A kee<sub>p</sub>s HL-26’s rule verbatim and changes only its 26 codewords, worth 6.26<sup>◦</sup>.

The alphabet’s content matters at either end: <sub>rep</sub>l<sub>ac</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26’<sub>s</sub> <sub>cu</sub>b<sub>e</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a-</sub>fit <sub>co</sub>d<sub>e</sub>b<sub>oo</sub>k <sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>same</sub> $K { = } 2 6$ and the same 4.70 fixed-width bits is worth $6 . 2 6 ^ { \circ }$ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>even aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>es</sub>t fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>geome</sub>t<sub>ry we cou</sub>ld b<sub>u</sub>ild (Fibonacci $\bar { K } { = } 1 2 8 , 6 . 9 6 ^ { \circ }$ at 5.47 entropy bits) a data-fit perbone code at matched rate is 1.32<sup>◦</sup> better. Why the two metrics order A and F diferently. Both <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>cs pu</sub>t HL<sub>-</sub>26 <sub>wors</sub>t <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th d<sub>eco</sub>d<sub>ers,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t th<sub>ey sp</sub>lit <sub>o</sub>n th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> Di<sub>g</sub>itC<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e va</sub>ri<sub>a</sub>nt<sub>s:</sub> AE<sub>-</sub>FGD <sub>p</sub>r<sub>e</sub>f<sub>e</sub>r<sub>s pe</sub>r<sub>-</sub>b<sub>o</sub>n<sub>e</sub> (3.01 vs. 3.77), speed-JS prefers per-finger (0.075 vs. 0.152). This is not noise (JS std ≤ 0.003; the AE-FGD gap is ≈3 <sub>p</sub>ooled stds) but a diference in what is measured. S<sub>p</sub>eed-JS is a marginal over one scalar; AE-FGD is a joint distance in a latent encoding pose and dynamics together. At K=64 the per-finger code registers more frame-to-frame change (44% vs. 34%; Fig. 7), so it resolves small velocities better, while its coarser pose resolution (7.10<sup>◦</sup> vs. 5.68<sup>◦</sup>; Table 29) puts its f<sub>rames</sub> f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>rea</sub>l <sub>man</sub>if<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>—</sub>th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>pu</sub>ll <sub>oppo-</sub> <sub>s</sub>it<sub>e</sub> <sub>ways.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> <sub>rea</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>or</sub>d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>o</sub>f AE<sub>-</sub>FGD<sub>,</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>nce</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>spee</sub>d mar<sub>g</sub>inal cannot detect ri<sub>g</sub>ht s<sub>p</sub>eeds with wron<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>oses (the colla<sub>p</sub>se-sensitivit<sub>y</sub> that retired the statistic-FGD; Metrics), <sub>an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>qua</sub>lifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on:</sub> <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>spee</sub>d <sub>marg</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l <sub>a</sub>l<sub>one</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>nger co</sub>d<sub>e genera</sub>t<sub>es</sub> b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er.</sub> Thi<sub>s s</sub>t<sub>u</sub>d<sub>y a</sub>l<sub>so</sub> h<sub>as</sub> th<sub>e sma</sub>ll<sub>es</sub>t n (16 AR clips/seed, 9 masked), where a Fréchet covariance i<sub>s</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> bi<sub>ase</sub>d<sub>—w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h i<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>y</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26 <sub>gap,</sub> l<sub>arge an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>i<sub>gn-cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>a</sub>t <sub>every see</sub>d <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> d<sub>eco</sub>d<sub>ers,</sub> i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>rawn</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> it <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>qua</sub>lifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

## K Whole-Hand (Top-Level) Tokens

B<sub>eyon</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>co</sub>d<sub>es,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>pro</sub>b<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>or</sub>th<sub>ogona</sub>l t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> t<sub>ypes:</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>o</sub>l<sub>e-</sub>h<sub>an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ape</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a</sub> <sub>cross-</sub>fi<sub>nger</sub> <sub>re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>l t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en.</sub>

Whole-hand shape token. A single k-means code over the full 60-D hand (20 bones × 3) gives one shape token per frame—the most compact, top-level code. On subjectdisjoint 137-class gesture classification (Table 25), combining a 128-entry whole-hand code with DigitCode-F lifts top-1 to 49.7% (+6.7pp over HL-26; Table 7) at 1.80 bits/vector, 55% of HL-26’s rate, and DigitCode-F alone matches HL-26 (43.0%) at 1.46 bits. A single whole-hand token is the most rate-eficient identity code by a wide margin: at K=256 it reaches 46.8% from one token per frame (0.38 bits/vector), above the 20-token per-bone code’s 46.2% at 14.6× the rate and above HL-26’s 43.0% at 8.6×.

## L Token Editing and Bimanual Preservation

O<sub>n</sub> I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>H<sub>an</sub>d2<sub>.</sub>6M t<sub>wo-</sub>h<sub>an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on we</sub> t<sub>es</sub>t <sub>a</sub> bi<sub>manua</sub>l edit: steer the right hand toward a target pose by a symb<sub>o</sub>li<sub>c</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en e</sub>dit<sub>,</sub> th<sub>en measure r</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>-</sub>h<sub>an</sub>d fid<sub>e</sub>lit<sub>y an</sub>d l<sub>e</sub>ft<sub>-</sub> hand disturbance, with joint = right\_match×left\_preserve $( \tau _ { \mathrm { r i g h t } } { = } 2 0 ^ { \circ } , \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { l e f t } } { = } 5 ^ { \circ }$ , sequence-level mean over 18 captures). We tabulate thefeasible slice (both hands valid); thefull slice (ri<sub>g</sub>ht hand valid onl<sub>y</sub>) <sub>g</sub>ives the same orderin<sub>g</sub>. Because a factored per-hand code rewrites only right-hand tokens, the l<sub>e</sub>ft h<sub>an</sub>d i<sub>s</sub> b<sub>y</sub>t<sub>e-</sub>f<sub>or-</sub>b<sub>y</sub>t<sub>e un</sub>t<sub>ouc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>, so</sub> it<sub>s</sub> d<sub>r</sub>ift i<sub>s prova</sub>bl<sub>y</sub> 0 for all factored codes—extending the single-hand locality <sub>o</sub>f Fi<sub>g.</sub> 10 t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> bi<sub>manua</sub>l <sub>case as a s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>guaran</sub>t<sub>ee,</sub> not a learned behavior. Among factored codes the joint score is then driven b<sub>y</sub> ri<sub>g</sub>ht-hand fidelit<sub>y</sub> (a rate–distortion <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>): DigitCode-H reproduces the target right hand to 2.2<sup>◦</sup> and <sub>p</sub>er-<sup>fi</sup>n<sub>g</sub>er to $7 . 3 ^ { \circ } .$ versus $1 4 . 7 ^ { \circ }$ f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e coarse</sub> HL<sub>-</sub>26<sub>, so</sub> Di<sub>g</sub>itCode-H attains the best joint score (Table 27). As a foil we add a non-factoredjoint code over both hands (256 entries on the 40-bone pose): steering its right half drags the left half <sup>b</sup><sub>y</sub> $3 0 . 9 ^ { \circ }$ and collapses preservation to 0.25. ## N Cross-Regime Generalization and Per-Finger Robustness

Additional regimes. We use two openly available datasets that <sub>p</sub>rovide 3D joints directl<sub>y</sub> (no MANO fittin<sub>g</sub>), avoidin<sub>g</sub> access and fittin<sub>g</sub> confounds: HanCo (Zimmermann, Argus, and Brox 2021) (1,455 free single-hand sequences, 102,359 frames) and ASL-Skeleton3D (de Amorim and Zanchettin 2022) (9,398 American Sign Language samples, 28,358 frames, with handshape/gloss/signer labels). ASL-Skeleton3D is a 2D→3D liftin<sub>g</sub> and is therefore nois<sub>y</sub>; its <sub>a</sub>b<sub>so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e errors are</sub> i<sub>n</sub>fl<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>an</sub>d <sub>we rea</sub>d it<sub>s num</sub>b<sub>ers as re</sub>l<sub>-</sub> <sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve compar</sub>i<sub>sons.</sub> E<sub>ac</sub>h d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t i<sub>s mappe</sub>d t<sub>o a common</sub> 21-joint topology by an identity map.

Distribution and within-regime R–D. Across all four regimes the direction distribution is anisotropic (+Y occupies 57–70%) and HL-26’s usage entropy is 2.9–3.6 bits, far below the log 26 = 4.70 ceiling, so a fixed code wastes <sub>sym</sub>b<sub>o</sub>l<sub>s</sub> <sub>everyw</sub>h<sub>ere.</sub> D<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a-a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>co</sub>d<sub>es</sub> <sub>w</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> <sub>every</sub> regime; on HanCo, k-means-64 reaches 4.22<sup>◦</sup> versus HL-26’s 14.91<sup>◦</sup>. Temporal-relative coding again lowers both rate <sub>an</sub>d <sub>error on</sub> H<sub>an</sub>C<sub>o, repro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>H<sub>an</sub>d <sub>resu</sub>lt<sub>.</sub>

Diagnosing per-finger collapse. Because DigitCode-F packs a finger’s four bones into one 12-D code, a single corrupted bone can hijack the shared assignment: finger error correlates with the single worst bone at 0.90–0.92, and the fraction of “hijacked” fingers rises 7× under noise (11.0% on ASL vs. 1.6% clean). Table 29 compares two fixes. A robust-assi<sub>g</sub>nment variant (I2; choose the centroid b<sub>y</sub> the best three bones) fails—it cannot help an of-manifold bone <sub>an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>li<sub>g</sub>htl<sub>y</sub> h<sub>ur</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Addi<sub>ng an</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>per-</sub>b<sub>one res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l on to<sub>p</sub> of the coarse <sub>p</sub>er-fin<sub>g</sub>er code (I1, i.e. Di<sub>g</sub>itCode-H) d<sub>ecoup</sub>l<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e corrup</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>one an</sub>d <sub>w</sub>i<sub>ns w</sub>ithi<sub>n-</sub>ASL <sub>an</sub>d <sub>on</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ean</sub> I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>H<sub>an</sub>d<sub>,</sub> b<sub>ea</sub>ti<sub>ng per-</sub>b<sub>one</sub> Di<sub>g</sub>itC<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e-</sub>A th<sub>ere; un-</sub> der <sub>p</sub>ure IH→ASL transfer Di<sub>g</sub>itCode-A remains mar<sub>g</sub>inall<sub>y</sub> better (8.14 vs. 8.95).

## O The Symbolic Repair Interface: Protocol and Full Results

Protocol. Held-out InterHand2.6M; DigitCode-F with K=128. All conclusions are re-verified at $K \_ { \mathbf { \Sigma } } \in$ {64, 128, 256} within $\pm 0 . 5 ^ { \circ }$ <sub>w</sub>ith d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> AUC rising monotonically (0.945/0.953/0.960 of-manifold, 0.808/0.823/0.827 illegal). We use two corruption regimes: of-manifold (each bone of a finger pushed $\sigma { = } 4 5 ^ { \circ }$ ; 400 poses × 5 fingers = 2000 slots) and anatomically illegal (PIP/DIP/TIP reflected about the MCP axis into h<sub>yp</sub>erext<sub>ens</sub>i<sub>on, app</sub>li<sub>e</sub>d <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>o cur</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d fi<sub>ngers w</sub>ith MCP<sub>–</sub>TIP <sub>ang</sub>l<sub>e</sub> $> 4 5 ^ { \circ } ; n { = } 8 4 1 )$ ). Metrics are corru<sub>p</sub>ted-fin<sub>g</sub>er and innocentfinger angular error; plausibility = 1-NN angular distance of the finger’s 12-D direction vector to a 4,000-pose pool of real trainin<sub>g</sub> fin<sub>g</sub>ers (codebook-inde<sub>p</sub>endent; <sub>g</sub>round-truth fin<sub>g</sub>ers score $8 . 3 ^ { \circ } ) ;$ and OOL = fraction of fingers whose plausibility exceeds the 99th percentile of clean fingers (27.1<sup>◦</sup>). OOL scores what a repair emits, so the unrepaired row carries <sub>no en</sub>t<sub>ry</sub> i<sub>n e</sub>ith<sub>er repa</sub>i<sub>r</sub> t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e.</sub> B<sub>ecause every sym</sub>b<sub>o</sub>li<sub>c arm</sub> l<sub>an</sub>d<sub>s a</sub>t $5 { - } 1 1 ^ { \circ }$ <sub>p</sub>l<sub>aus</sub>ibilit<sub>y across</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo reg</sub>i<sub>mes,</sub> f<sub>ar</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>id<sub>e</sub> the threshold, OOL is 0 for all of them by a wide margin. Th<sub>e co</sub>l<sub>umn</sub>’<sub>s con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> MANO <sub>re</sub>fit l<sub>eaves</sub> th<sub>e</sub> manifold at scale (0.82–0.94; the learned continuous inpaint grazes it at 0.01 on the illegal regime until the codebook projection zeroes it), and that a codeword-emitting repair cannot leave it at all. Detection thresholds τ are percentiles of the <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ean res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

Snap restores validity but not correctness; the whole-hand VQ shows the cross-fin<sub>g</sub>er context si<sub>g</sub>nal but is <sub>no</sub>t <sub>a</sub>dd<sub>ressa</sub>bl<sub>e;</sub> MANO <sub>arms</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>.</sub>

Detection operating points. Of-manifold regime: τ=90th pct (19.4<sup>◦</sup>) gives recall 0.88 at 10% false positives; 95th (22.7<sup>◦</sup>): 0.79/0.05; 99th $( 3 6 . 3 ^ { \circ } ) : 0 . 3 3 / 0 . 0 \dot { 1 }$ <sub>.</sub> O<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> harder illegal regime the τ=90 gate recalls 0.50; see the <sub>repa</sub>i<sub>r</sub> <sub>paragrap</sub>h b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ow.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>or</sub> <sub>compar</sub>i<sub>son,</sub> AUC <sub>pa</sub>i<sub>rs are repor</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as o</sub>f<sub>-man</sub>if<sub>o</sub>ld / ill<sub>ega</sub>l<sub>: co</sub>d<sub>ewor</sub>d residual 0.953 / 0.823; trained Mahalanobis 0.961 / 0.684; intra-finger incoherence 0.737 / 0.615; MANO-refit residual 0.428 / 0.534. Read down the pairs by detector rather than across a single regime: the trained detector buys 0.008 of-manifold and gives back 0.139 on illegal poses, while th<sub>e</sub> MANO<sub>-re</sub>fit <sub>res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l i<sub>s</sub> b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ow</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>ance</sub> <sub>o</sub>f<sub>-man</sub>if<sub>o</sub>ld <sub>an</sub>d barely above it on illegal poses (0.428, 0.534)—refitting a <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t h<sub>as no no</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> l<sub>ega</sub>l <sub>se</sub>t <sub>canno</sub>t <sub>measure</sub> di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ance</sub> t<sub>o one.</sub> O<sub>n</sub>l<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e co</sub>d<sub>ewor</sub>d <sub>res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l i<sub>s</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>rong</sub> i<sub>n</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>co</sub>l<sub>umns, an</sub>d it i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e on</sub>l<sub>y one</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>nee</sub>d<sub>s no</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub>

Nameable codes. Codes carry zero-training names: semantic naming by majority ASL handshape (signer-disjoint: 166/256 codes named, purity 0.32, coverage 0.97) makes name→code retrieval reach 26.8% vs. a 10.1% majority baseline (44 classes) by pure table lookup (e.g. code 246 [allcurl] is the ASL “10”/fist, 232 [all-ext] is “5”). The codebook <sub>mus</sub>t <sub>s</sub>h<sub>are</sub> th<sub>e name</sub>d d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a</sub>’<sub>s canon</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>pa</sub>l<sub>m</sub> f<sub>rame—</sub>fitti<sub>ng</sub> in the world frame drops retrieval to 18.4%.

## P Embodied Retargeting and Label Screening: Protocol and Per-Hand Tables

Protocol. Retargeting uses a standard position optimizer (Qin et al. 2023) over six ri<sub>g</sub>ht robot hands s<sub>p</sub>annin<sub>g</sub> the kinematic desi<sub>g</sub>n s<sub>p</sub>ace: Alle<sub>g</sub>ro and Lea<sub>p</sub> (inde<sub>p</sub>endent fin<sub>g</sub>er chains), Shadow and Schunk SVH (fin<sub>g</sub>ers sharin<sub>g</sub> wrist joints), and Abilit<sub>y</sub> and Ins<sub>p</sub>ire (underactuated, mimiccou<sub>p</sub>led). Looku<sub>p</sub> arms and their <sub>p</sub>er-frame reference are compared under the same frozen 6-DoF base (goldFB: frozen-base <sub>p</sub>er-frame o<sub>p</sub>timization), so the com<sub>p</sub>arison is <sub>pa</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d <sub>an</sub>d b<sub>ase-ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>; a per-</sub>h<sub>an</sub>d <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s ca</sub>lib<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>once</sub> on 30 reference frames in the same frozen-base regime. H<sub>uman</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>canon</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l<sub>-</sub>f<sub>rame</sub> F<sub>re</sub>iHAND <sub>poses;</sub> <sub>co</sub>d<sub>e-</sub> books (DigitCode-F, K=128 per finger; whole-hand codes at 256/640/4096 entries for budget-matched contrasts) are fit on the training partition only. Evaluation uses n=800 held-out frames, 3 codebook seeds $( \mathrm { s t d } \leq 0 . 1 1 \mathrm { m m } )$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d f<sub>rame-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l paired bootstrap 95% CIs. Task error is the mean fingertip <sub>p</sub>osition error (mm) of the executed robot <sub>p</sub>ose a<sub>g</sub>ainst the <sup>retar</sup>g<sup>etin</sup>g <sup>tar</sup>g<sup>ets</sup>.

Compiled lookup across six hands. Compiling the perfinger codebook costs 640 IK solves (5 fingers × 128 codes, 1.6 s, once); streaming retargeting is then O(1) table assembly at ∼0.005 ms/frame against 2.4–7.5 ms/frame for perframe optimization, a 480–1500× amortized speedup that h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>s on every</sub> h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>.</sub> A <sub>per-</sub>b<sub>one co</sub>d<sub>e canno</sub>t b<sub>e comp</sub>il<sub>e</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub>ll<sub>:</sub> b<sub>one co</sub>d<sub>es</sub> d<sub>o no</sub>t <sub>compose a</sub> fi<sub>nger-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l IK t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>,</sub> <sub>so</sub> th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>eco</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub> <sub>s</sub>till <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res</sub> <sub>per-</sub>f<sub>rame</sub> <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> (2.5 ms/frame on Allegro). Table 32 reports accuracy. On All<sub>egro</sub> th<sub>e per-</sub>fi<sub>nger</sub> l<sub>oo</sub>k<sub>up ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>es</sub> f<sub>rozen-</sub>b<sub>ase per-</sub>f<sub>rame</sub> optimization within 0.4 mm in this sweep (Table 32; 0.7 mm under an earlier calibration); across the other hands a wholeh<sub>an</sub>d t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e a</sub>t <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>or</sub> l<sub>arger</sub> b<sub>u</sub>d<sub>ge</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e more accura</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>comp</sub>il<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>un</sub>it<sub>,</sub> <sub>cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> <sub>paper</sub>’<sub>s</sub> <sub>cross-</sub>fi<sub>nger-</sub> correlation evidence (real hand <sub>p</sub>oses concentrate on a manifold that a few hundred whole-hand <sub>p</sub>rotot<sub>yp</sub>es cover well). This is the granularity-to-task mapping on the robot: the finger is the unit that gives addressability (editing one finger re-looks-up only that finger’s joints) and the screening interface below, the whole hand is the more accurate compilation unit, and the bone compiles to nothing.

O), and detect-and-dro<sub>p</sub> on a mixed clean/corru<sub>p</sub>t stream collapses label error (∆q 0.033 → 0.002 on Allegro, 0.024 → 0.003 on Shadow) at the cost of discardin 52% of f<sub>rames—repa</sub>i<sub>r vs.</sub> d<sub>rop</sub> i<sub>s a</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a-scarc</sub>it<sub>y</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on.</sub>
